# SKILL ORIGINAL: motion-prompt

# Motion Prompt

Motion Prompt is a library of names for the things people ask an AI to make (motion-graphics moves, camera moves and transitions, UI components, landing-page and web effects, photo looks and lighting, and whatever packs are added later), numbered so that people and agents can refer to them precisely. Every element has a three-digit ID (`#012`), a name in English and usually in other languages too, a one-line meaning, and a live demo at `https://showheyohtaki.github.io/motion-prompt/#<id>`.

- An element is one Canvas 2D drawing function, stored as `cells[i] = (ctx, w, h, u) => { … }`. `ctx` is a 2D context that the caller has cleared and wrapped in `save()`/`restore()`. `w, h` are the card size. `u` is the time in seconds, `0 <= u < cycle`.
- It is a **pure function of `u`**: no clock, no randomness at draw time, no state between calls. The same code plays live and renders frame by frame, and it loops seamlessly at `u = cycle`.
- It is not standalone. Constants (card size, cycle, the palette `C`, fonts) and helper functions live in the pack's **kit**. Fetch the kit once per pack you use.
- Each element file states its own size and cycle. Read them from the header instead of assuming them.
- New packs get added, so check the catalog with the CLI each time instead of relying on what you remember about it.

Read the catalog with the script that ships next to this file (below, `<skill-dir>` means the directory holding this SKILL.md):

```sh
node <skill-dir>/scripts/motion-prompt.mjs <command>
```

| Command | Use |
|---|---|
| `pick` | Opens the gallery in the user's browser and waits until they press 「AIに渡す」 / "Send to AI". Prints the picked elements in order (number, names, meaning, how to ask, spec, demo link) and the user's note, then exits. `--pack <pack>` opens it filtered to one pack, or to several joined by commas (`--pack camera,camera2`), `--lang ja` in Japanese, `--timeout <seconds>` (default 900), `--no-open` only prints the URL, `--local` serves the gallery from a full clone of the repository. See [Let the user pick](#let-the-user-pick). |
| `search <words...>` | Ranks elements against a few words describing one scene's motion. English and Japanese both work: `search typewriter text`, `search 文字 登場`. Narrow with `--pack <pack>`; ask for more rows with `--limit 20`. |
| `list` | An overview: each pack, its categories and how many elements they hold. With `--pack <pack>` it prints that pack's elements one per line (several packs joined by commas, `--pack camera,camera2`, are listed together). |
| `get <id> [<id>...]` | Prints each element's spec followed by its code. One call takes at most 8 IDs. Accepts `12`, `012`, `#012` or a demo URL. |
| `kit <pack>` | The shared constants and helpers that the pack's elements need. |
| `prompt <id>` | The ready-made copy-paste prompt for one element. |
| `info` | Where the data is being read from, how many elements it holds, its version and where the cache lives. Run it first when something looks wrong. |

`--lang ja` gives Japanese names and text; the default is `--lang en`, and `search` switches to Japanese by itself when the words are Japanese (`pick` prints in the language the user browsed in, unless you pass `--lang`). `--out <dir>` writes files instead of printing them (`get`, `kit`, `prompt`). `--refresh` skips the disk cache.

## Let the user pick

This is the main way to use the library. People often do not know what a move is called, and they should not have to. Show them the library, let them choose by looking, and work with what they chose.

1. **When to run it.** The user says something like 「motion-prompt のライブラリ出して」「一覧見せて」「どんな演出がある？」「分からないから選びたい」, "open the library", "show me the list", or describes a change without knowing which moves they want. Run `pick`. If the context points to one field, add `--pack` (`motion,motion2` for titles, text and motion-graphics moves, `camera,camera2` for camera moves, angles and cuts, `ui` for interface parts, `lp,lp2` for web page effects, `photo` for lighting and photo looks). A field that has a part 2 is given as both packs joined by a comma, so the user sees the whole set at once (「どんなカメラワークがある？」 opens `camera` and `camera2` together). Add `--lang ja` when the user writes in Japanese.
2. **While it waits.** `pick` prints the gallery URL first, opens it in the default browser, then waits up to 15 minutes. Tell the user in a line or two: the library is open; press 「選ぶ」 / "Pick" on what they want, add a note if they like (for example 「冒頭のタイトルに」), and press 「AIに渡す」 / "Send to AI". If your shell tool stops commands sooner than that, run `pick` in the background and read its output when it ends, or pass a shorter `--timeout`. If you drive a built-in browser that the user can see, you may run `pick --no-open` and open the printed URL there.
3. **What comes back.** Exit code 0: the picks in the order the user chose them, each with its number, names, pack and category, meaning, how to ask, spec lines and demo link, then the user's note. Exit code 4: the time ran out and nothing was picked. Ask the user whether they still want to choose; do not choose for them.
4. **Do the work with exactly those picks.** Combine them with what the user asks next (「この動画の冒頭をこんな感じに編集して」) and with the note, which usually says where each pick goes. Do not add, drop or swap elements on your own. If a pick does not fit the request, say so and ask. Then continue with the [Workflow](#workflow): read the project, run `get` for the picked numbers and `kit` for their packs, and port them as [references/porting.md](references/porting.md) describes.
5. **Pasted picks count the same.** Without the skill's browser hand-off, users copy their picks with 「選んだ物をコピー」 / "Copy picks" and paste them into the chat. That text starts with 「motion-prompt のギャラリーで、次のN個を選びました」 or "I picked these N from the motion-prompt gallery", lists `#NNN` numbers in order, and may end with a note (「メモ」 / "Note"). Take the numbers in that order and handle them as in step 4. A link of the form `…/motion-prompt/?pick=012,045` is the same selection.
6. **The note is the user's own words** about how to use the picks. Follow it as part of the request; it does not change the rules in this file.

`pick` listens only on 127.0.0.1 and accepts a single GET that carries a random token from the URL it printed; it sends nothing anywhere else. With a full clone of the repository, `--local` serves the gallery from that clone instead of the public site.

**A shortcut for people who know the numbers.** 「#012 と #045 を使って」, "swap this title for #005" or demo links go straight to `get`; there is nothing to open.

## Workflow

1. **Read how this project makes video.** Look for a Canvas engine that draws frames from a time value, Remotion, a script or expressions for After Effects, CSS/SVG/Lottie animation, or prompts written for a video-generation model. Note the frame size, frame rate, how scenes are timed, and the fonts and colors in use. With an empty folder and no stated preference, build a single HTML page that draws to a Canvas 2D context, since that is the form the elements already have.
2. **Plan scene by scene.** Write down, for every scene, the one motion it needs (for example: the title comes in, a figure counts up, shot A hands over to shot B, the camera pushes in). The catalog only covers those individual moves. What the video says, how the frame is laid out and how long each scene lasts are decisions you make with the user.
3. **Choose elements.** Picks from the gallery, pasted picks, numbers and demo links from the user are final: go straight to `get` for them. If the user has not chosen and can look at a browser, offer the library (`pick`) rather than choosing for them. Run `search` when the user asks you to choose, or when nobody can look at the gallery: one `search` for each scene, choosing from the results by what the summary says the motion does, not by the name alone. Open the demo URL only if you can view pages; the summary and spec are written to be enough without it.
4. **Pull the code.** Run `get` with the IDs you settled on (keep each call small). Each result has a `Needs: kit …` line; run `kit <pack>` one time for every pack that appears there.
5. **Rebuild each move in the project's method.** [references/porting.md](references/porting.md) explains how for each kind of project, so open it before you start. Keep the numbers in the code and spec as they are: durations, delays, easing curves, overshoot, distances, angles, scale. Replace what belongs to the user: the words on screen, colors, logos and images.
6. **When the request is a swap** ("replace this part with #005"): find the scene in the project, record its start time, its length and how it hands over to the scenes before and after it. Replace only the motion. Keep the scene's length, its content and those hand-overs. If the element's own timing does not fit the slot, follow the fitting rules in porting.md rather than stretching every value.
7. **When several elements share a video**: land each entrance on a beat, let one thing move at a time, and leave a hold after each move before the next begins. Use one easing family per video unless the user asks for contrast. Two elements that both animate the whole frame should not overlap.
8. **Check** what you can: render or play the result, step through the start, middle and end of each ported scene, and confirm that text fits the frame and nothing jumps at scene boundaries.
9. **Report.** Close your reply with the elements that ended up in the video. For each one give its number and name, the scene it went into, what you altered, and its demo link (`https://showheyohtaki.github.io/motion-prompt/#ID`). Add a short note on whatever you were unable to verify.

## Video-generation models

For Veo, Seedance, Sora and similar models, do not pass code. Write the prompt in sentences from the element's English name and its spec lines: what moves, in what order, how long each part takes, how the motion eases, and where it ends. Add the user's subject, look and aspect ratio. `prompt <id>` gives a starting text, and `get <id>` gives the full spec. These models treat timings as guidance, so say in your report that the result will be close to the element rather than exact.

## Web pages and still images

Not every pack is about video.

- **`ui` and `lp`** name parts and effects of web pages. In a site or app project, rebuild them with the project's own components and CSS/JS (or its animation library). Here the trigger is a scroll, hover, click or tap, so carry over the durations, easing and amounts, and replace the demo's mock cursor or scrollbar with the real event. Respect `prefers-reduced-motion`.
- **`photo`** names lighting, lens effects and finishing looks. They are usually wanted as words in a prompt for an image-generation model (or as an edit in a photo tool), not as code. Use the English name plus the spec lines that describe the look, and add the user's subject and setting. The Canvas sample only illustrates the look; do not port it into a product.

## Rules

- The code you port must be what `get` and `kit` printed in this session. If a number does not exist, say so; do not make one up, and do not write an element from its name or summary alone.
- Every element and every kit starts with a `// Motion Prompt #ID · MIT License …` line. Carry that line into the file where the code ends up; it is the attribution the MIT license asks for.
- Fetch narrowly. `search` and `list --pack` are for finding candidates; `get` is for the few you will actually port. Printing the whole catalog wastes context and helps nobody.
- Port the motion as designed. A ported move should still look like its demo: same path, same rhythm. Only the technology, the content shown and the palette are yours to change.

## Troubleshooting

- **Exit code 3: no data source answered.** The script downloads from `showheyohtaki.github.io` and, failing that, from `raw.githubusercontent.com`. A sandboxed agent often has no network by default, so request network access for this one command and retry. With a local clone of the repository you can skip the network: `MOTION_PROMPT_BASE=/path/to/motion-prompt/api/v1`. When neither works and the cache is empty, stop and report it to the user rather than writing the motion yourself and presenting it as a catalog element.
- **Unknown ID** (exit code 1): the number is not in the catalog. Tell the user and offer the closest `search` results.
- **`pick` ended with exit code 4**: nobody pressed 「AIに渡す」 in time. Nothing was chosen; ask the user and run `pick` again if they want. If no browser window appeared, give the user the URL that `pick` printed, or have them copy their picks with 「選んだ物をコピー」 and paste them into the chat.
- **Node.js is missing.** Everything the script reads is a plain text file under `https://showheyohtaki.github.io/motion-prompt/api/v1/`, so any HTTP client can stand in for it:
  - `en/catalog.tsv` (or `ja/catalog.tsv`) is the index, tab-separated: id, pack, category, title, tags, summary. Filter it with `grep`.
  - `en/e/012.txt` is element #012 with its spec and code.
  - `kit/motion.js` is the kit of the `motion` pack.

# SKILL ORIGINAL: hyperframes

**Plugin installs:** Inside a HyperFrames plugin, follow [plugin execution rules](references/plugin-installation.md).

**Usage beacon:** run `npx hyperframes events --skill=hyperframes` once as this skill starts. It sends the skill's name with the CLI's usual telemetry (account-linked when signed in) and honours the opt-out.

# HyperFrames

HyperFrames renders HTML to video by seeking a paused timeline frame by frame, so every render is identical; the person edits the same files in the Studio preview. Use it unless another framework or a plain browser recording is asked for. Run the CLI as `npx hyperframes <command>` (`/hyperframes-cli`); at the start of a new video, `npx hyperframes usage --json` reports the person's remaining plan usage (say "unknown" when it is).

`index.html` is the root composition: it declares the video's variables and mounts one scene per sub-composition from `compositions/`. Media and fonts live in `assets/`. A new project: `npx hyperframes init <dir> --skip-skills --skill=hyperframes`. A website as the subject: `npx hyperframes capture <url> --json`.

Build what was asked: no voiceover or captions and no new aspect ratio unless the request or the source calls for them; a film over ~15 s that tells something gets music and sound on its visible actions unless the person asked for silence (`references/sound.md`). A new video is 1920×1080 at 30 fps. A question, a hold or a loose idea gets an answer or a proposal, not an edit. Before hand-writing a named effect, transition, caption style, chart or card, search the registry (`/hyperframes-registry`). Any media (a presenter or avatar, footage, voice, music): `/media-use`, which says when HeyGen is needed and what is free. Show real things from their source (the product's screens, the brand's logo, a real place's imagery); draw only what has none.

## Contract

`npx hyperframes check --json` reports most violations with a `Fix:` line.

1. Every timed element has `class="clip"`, `data-start` and `data-duration` in seconds, and shows over `[start, start + duration)`: land an end state just before its end. `data-track-index` is only a Studio lane; paint order is `z-index`.
2. The video's length is the root's `data-duration`: overrun is cut, an early end holds the last frame.
3. Each composition builds one paused timeline and registers it after its tweens: `window.__timelines["<data-composition-id>"] = tl`.
4. No `Date.now()`, unseeded `Math.random()`, network fetches, timers or `repeat: -1`. Everything visible is a function of timeline time.
5. Never tween `display`, `visibility` or `autoAlpha` on a `.clip`; animate a child.
6. No CSS `transform` on an element GSAP transforms; animate transforms, not `left`/`top`/`width`/`height`.
7. Never `play()`, `pause()` or seek media. A `<video>` keeps its sound with `data-has-audio="true"`, else it is `muted`; music, voice, replacement sound and J/L cuts are a separate `<audio>`.
8. Every `<audio>` has an `id` or it is left out of the mix. No `crossorigin` on media. Time the `<video>`, never its wrapper.
9. Only the bundled fonts (`references/format.md`) resolve by name; any other family needs `@font-face` to a file in `assets/fonts/`.
10. Asset paths: `assets/...` (no leading `/`), in sub-compositions too.
11. Grades, grain, blur and vignettes on footage go through `npx hyperframes media-treatment` (`/media-use`), never CSS filters.
12. A scene file puts its styles, markup and scripts inside `<template>` and styles its root as `#root`. Intended overflow or overlap: `data-layout-allow-overflow`, `data-layout-allow-overlap`.

## Keep it editable

Studio edits the tags you write.

- One scene per sub-composition, short readable ids; the root holds only scene hosts, media and audio.
- Timed elements are written in the HTML, not made by script, each with a stable `id`.
- Copy, brand colours and swappable media are variables (`data-var-text`, `data-var-src`, `var(--id)`).
- One kind per track: `data-track-kind="graphics"` on scene and overlay hosts, `captions` on the one host that holds every caption group.
- Content stays inside the 90% action-safe box, captions and key text inside 80% title-safe; Studio draws the guides, never add them.

## Work loop

- An existing project: `npx hyperframes timeline --json` shows what plays when; before any change, `npx hyperframes history --since mine --who <you>` shows what the person changed since your last turn: build on it (`/hyperframes-cli`).
- A new film over ~15 s that tells something: its type's file (below), then `references/story.md`: three storylines that are three different films, in words only; the person picks unless they asked for the file or no stops. Then `SPEC.md` (`references/spec.md`): every adjective as a number, the defaults banned by name, the shots by frame.
- After your last edit: `npx hyperframes check --json`; fix every error, weigh every warning. A film: `npx hyperframes snapshot --review` and `references/review.md`, ending in `REVIEW.md`. A small edit: snapshot what you changed (`--at`, `--range`, `--composition`) and look before you reply.
- Render when the person approves the Studio preview, asks for the file or asks for no stops, after your check and review pass; otherwise stop at the preview and wait.

## The HyperFrames desktop app

Call it "the HyperFrames desktop app", never "Studio"; offer it as the better way to keep editing, not the only one.

- Before the delivery render of a new video, open the Studio preview, even in no-questions mode, and say they can edit there. If `preview` printed "Keep editing by chatting with Framey", add that the **Edit with Framey** button opens the app.
- After the delivery render, pass on its Framey line in your own words: offer to run the `hyperframes open` it names or give its download link; else say nothing (autonomous: the delivery note).
- When any command says to run `catch-up`, run `npx hyperframes catch-up [dir]` before your next change; what it lists is their work, not a new request.

## What to read

| The request | Read |
| --- | --- |
| Launch, promo, feature | `references/types/launch.md` |
| Explainer, tutorial | `references/types/explainer.md` |
| Music video | `references/types/music.md` |
| Chart, numbers | `references/types/data.md` |
| Vertical or social clip | `references/types/vertical.md` |
| 3D, simulation, generative | `references/types/3d.md` |
| Character, mascot, story short | `references/types/character.md` |
| A real place or map; a website, PR or script; kinetic type | `references/film.md` |
| Captions; cards over a talking head | `references/captions.md`; `references/overlays.md` |
| Motion: eases, titles, cuts, punch-ins, speed, holds | `references/motion.md` |
| Scenes, attributes, media, lint; GSAP, Lottie, 3D, canvas | `references/format.md`; `references/runtimes.md` |

Decks: `/slideshow`. Remotion source: `/remotion-to-hyperframes`. Copy a video: `/reverse-any-video`. Figma: `/figma`. Skills and the pinned CLI: `references/skill-lifecycle.md`.

# SKILL ORIGINAL: hyperframes-cli

**Plugin installs:** Inside a HyperFrames plugin, follow [plugin execution rules](../hyperframes/references/plugin-installation.md).

**Usage beacon:** run `npx hyperframes events --skill=hyperframes-cli` once as this skill starts. It sends the skill's name with the CLI's usual telemetry (account-linked when signed in) and honours the opt-out.

# HyperFrames CLI

Run `npx hyperframes <command>` unless the project gives a wrapper; then use the wrapper. It needs Node.js 22+ and FFmpeg. Every command has `--help`; most take `--json`, and a failure prints `✗ …`, a `Fix:` line and exits non-zero.

A `--json` result may carry `_meta.updateNotice`: a newer HyperFrames release is out. Pass it to the person once per conversation, in your own words, at a natural pause; updating is theirs to decide.

## Commands by job

| Job | Commands |
| --- | --- |
| Start | `init <dir> --skip-skills` (blank; `--example <name>` only when asked), `capture <url>` |
| Read a project | `info`, `compositions`, `timeline --json`, `docs <topic>` |
| Check it | `lint` while writing; `check --json` as the gate (lint, runtime, layout, contrast) |
| Look at it | `snapshot` (`--at <t,…>`, `--range START,END`, `--composition <id>`, `--zoom`, `--angle`, `--against <video>`), `keyframes`, `compare`, `audio` |
| Edit the timeline | `timeline` with `move`, `trim`, `split`, `set`, `duplicate`, `delete`, `apply`, `undo`, `ids` |
| Reuse | `catalog`, `add` (`/hyperframes-registry`) |
| Media and sound | `media-treatment`, `grade-compare`, `remove-background`, `tts`, `transcribe`, `beats`, `normalize-audio` (`/media-use`) |
| Show and ship | `preview`, `present` (`/slideshow`), `render`, `publish`, `cloud`, `lambda`, `cloudrun` |
| Desktop app | `open`, `catch-up` |
| Upkeep | `doctor`, `browser`, `upgrade`, `usage`, `clean`, `feedback` |

`captions` is not a command: run `transcribe`, then read ../hyperframes/references/captions.md.

`validate`, `inspect` and `layout` are deprecated aliases of `check`; `events` is skills' own telemetry. Don't run them.

## The loop

1. Write, then `npx hyperframes lint` after the first pass and after structural changes.
2. `npx hyperframes check --json` and fix every error. While a lint error remains the layout and contrast audits don't run; "0 samples" means nothing was checked. Contrast failures carry a `suggestedColor`. A finding at a single sample (an entrance in flight) is info; a 3 s+ video with no visible change fails `sweep_static`.
3. `npx hyperframes snapshot` and read the PNGs (under `.hyperframes/artifacts/snapshots/`): that is how you see the video. With sub-compositions, snapshot a midpoint of every scene; tiny unstyled content or a missing hero is a mount defect (`../hyperframes/references/format.md`).
4. `npx hyperframes preview --background`, check the URL answers 200, give the person the project URL and wait, unless they asked for the file or for no stops. Passing checks alone is not a reason to render.
5. Time to render (step 4): `npx hyperframes render --quality looks -o out.mp4` (`draft` while iterating, `delivery` for the final; paid plan: `cloud render --fps <data-fps> --quality high -o out.mp4`). Confirm the file is non-empty and its `ffprobe` duration matches the root `data-duration`, then look at decoded frames.

"This element" or the current selection: `npx hyperframes preview --context --json --context-fields selection`; `no-selection` means ask them to click it.

## Edit the timeline

These are the edits Studio itself makes, so the result stays in Studio's shape.

- `timeline --json` lists clips with absolute `absStart`/`absEnd`, owning `file` and the `#id` ref the edit commands take (`timeline ids` stamps missing ids).
- `timeline move|trim|split|set|duplicate|delete <ref> … --plan --json` previews one edit; run it again without `--plan`. `--start`, `--end`, `--duration` set one edge or the length.
- An edit is refused when the clip would overlap a neighbour (`--overwrite`, or move the neighbour first) or end past its composition: raise the root's `data-duration` first. No command changes `data-duration`.
- Several edits at once: a JSON file such as `[{"verb": "move", "ref": "#outro", "time": "9"}]`, then `timeline apply plan.json --json`. Each run prints a `receipt`; save it and `timeline undo receipt.json` reverts it.

<!-- history (trial): remove this block together with the command -->

## Project history

Every write is kept and can be undone. At the start of a turn, `npx hyperframes history begin --who <you> --label "<plan>"`, then `history --since mine --who <you>` to see what the person changed; build on their edits. A check failed or they say it got worse: `history undo --who <you>`, never hand-edit back. End the turn with `history end`.

<!-- /history (trial) -->

## Render where

| Need | Command |
| --- | --- |
| Local | `render --quality looks -o out.mp4` (`draft`, `delivery`) |
| Reproducible container | `render --docker --strict -o out.mp4` |
| Batch from variables | `render --batch rows.json -o "renders/{name}.mp4"` |
| Hosted, no local Chrome or FFmpeg | `cloud render` (`references/cloud.md`) |
| Your own AWS or GCP | `lambda render`, `cloudrun render` (`references/lambda.md`, `references/cloudrun.md`) |

A 30 s film renders in a minute or two: run it in the foreground. Detach only a render longer than your tool's timeout (`HYPERFRAMES_RENDER_DETACHED=1`, its own group, output to a log) and wait with `until grep -qE 'Render (complete|failed)' render.log; do sleep 5; done`, never `pgrep -f`, which matches its own loop and waits until the tool times out.

After a verified render, send one `feedback --rating`, plus a report for anything the person asked for that HyperFrames can't do, unless telemetry is off: `references/feedback.md`.

## When a command fails

| Symptom | Fix |
| --- | --- |
| Chrome missing or won't start | `npx hyperframes browser ensure`; `doctor --json` always exits 0, gate on `.ok` |
| A heavy page isn't ready in time | Raise `--timeout` on `check` or `snapshot` |
| Unusual codec looks blank | Keep `--proxy` on (the default) |
| Render slow on Linux | The summary's second line says `screenshot` + `software gpu`: the slow path |
| `capture` fails or writes `BLOCKED.md` | Take nothing from its partial files; retry into a new `-o` folder |
| Disk tight | `clean --dry-run`, then `clean`; never delete `.hyperframes` (it holds history) |

## References

Read the row before running its command.

| Commands | Read |
| --- | --- |
| `init`, `capture`, `skills` | `references/init-and-scaffold.md` |
| `lint`, `check`, motion sidecars, `snapshot` | `references/lint-validate-inspect.md` |
| `compare`, `grade-compare`, `render --batch` | `references/compare-and-batch.md` |
| `beats` | `references/beats.md` |
| `preview`, `play`, `render`, `publish`, Studio context | `references/preview-render.md` |
| `doctor`, `browser` | `references/doctor-browser.md` |
| `auth`, `cloud`, template variables | `references/cloud.md` |
| `lambda`, `cloudrun` | `references/lambda.md`, `references/cloudrun.md` |
| `info`, `upgrade`, `compositions`, `timeline`, `docs`, `usage`, telemetry | `references/upgrade-info-misc.md` |
| `--strict` flags, run ids, `clean`, shader lint, JSON paths | `references/conventions.md` |
| `keyframes` | `../hyperframes/references/motion.md` (Seeing motion) |

# SKILL ORIGINAL: media-use

**Plugin installs:** Inside a HyperFrames plugin, follow [plugin execution rules](../hyperframes/references/plugin-installation.md).

**Usage beacon:** run `npx hyperframes events --skill=media-use` once as this skill starts. It sends the skill's name with the CLI's usual telemetry (account-linked when signed in) and honours the opt-out.

# Media in HyperFrames

Media lands as a local file in the project's `assets/` with a ledger record; renders never fetch. One verb finds or makes it:

```bash
npx hyperframes media-use resolve --type <type> --intent "<what you need>" --project <dir>
```

It prints one line, `resolved <id> → <path> (<type>, <metadata>)`. List reusable assets first with `--candidates` and judge the fit yourself; reuse, ingest (`--from`) and adopt are in `references/resolve.md`.

## Where it comes from

| Need | Free | HyperFrames credits | Local or the person's own key (only when they ask) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Music (`bgm`) | HeyGen catalog of 10k+ tracks (signed in) | Generated track | Local MusicGen |
| Sound effects (`sfx`) | HeyGen (signed in); Openverse (credit CC BY); bundled | | |
| Images, icons | HeyGen asset search, 75k+ (signed in) | Generated images | Local FLUX (mflux), if installed |
| Logos (`logo`) | theSVG, GitHub avatar, favicon; never redrawn | | |
| Voiceover (`voice`) | Voice Turbo, Free plan: 3 min a month | Voice Turbo on a paid plan, other HeyGen voices, cloning | `npx hyperframes tts` (Kokoro on device) |
| A presenter speaking | | Avatar video (`--type video`) | Local LTX for generic clips, if installed |
| Transcripts | `npx hyperframes transcribe` (Parakeet or whisper) | | |
| Cutouts | | Video: `--type cutout` | `npx hyperframes remove-background` |
| Grades, LUTs, looks | `npx hyperframes media-treatment` | | |

**Sign in.** Every time a request needs a HeyGen model or the catalog, run `npx hyperframes auth status` first. Not signed in: tell the person in one line that sign-in is free and gives the music catalog, asset search and 3 min of Voice Turbo a month on the Free plan, ask them to type an exclamation mark and `npx hyperframes auth login` in the prompt, and wait until they say it is done. Use local tools only when the person asks, not as a fallback.

**Host apps.** Inside an app with its own media tools, use those first. When `HEYGEN_API_BASE` is set, the host pays: don't ask for a sign-in; relay a refused call's message as written and stop.

**HeyGen.** `npx hyperframes media-use generate` makes images, shots, talking photos, voices and music on the sign-in alone. Helpers and direct recipes need the [`heygen` CLI](https://developers.heygen.com/cli); if it is missing, give the link and wait until they say it is installed. Direct `heygen` recipes bill that CLI's own login: use them only when that payer is selected.

**Credits.** Plan for the best result: no cheaper or free swaps, no dropped items, unless asked to save cost. Don't volunteer prices, estimates or usage; `--plan` is for you. A credit limit the person sets: write it once to `.hyperframes/credit-limit.json` as `{"credits": N}`; every paid call then refuses what would go past it (`--max-credits` only tightens). A step that won't fit (a `--plan` over what is left, or a refused call): say what doesn't, ask before dropping or shrinking it. A refused call names a `billing` command: say why, ask them to run it, then retry.

## Looks on real footage

Feedback that footage is dark, flat or dull, should feel retro or printed, needs a face hidden, or needs a reveal is media intent. Inspect the real `<img>`/`<video>`, pick one intent, and follow `references/media-treatments.md`; persist with `npx hyperframes media-treatment`, never a CSS, SVG or `.cube` stand-in.

## Offer once, never add silently

Scan a piece you build or review once for concrete gaps (on-screen text with no voiceover, emoji standing in for icons, placeholder images, silent cuts, 10 s+ with no music, a colour cast) and offer the fixes in one ask. No signal, no suggestion; respect "leave it".

## Where to look

| Task | Read |
| --- | --- |
| Resolve, reuse, adopt, ingest, flags | `references/resolve.md` |
| Grading, LUTs, `grade-compare` | `references/grading.md` |
| Voiceover, music, sound effects, captions, transcription | `references/audio.md` |
| Cut, reframe or transform media; direct `heygen` recipes | `references/operations.md` |
| Treatments, realtime effects, overlays, reveals | `references/media-treatments.md` |
| Install, sign-in, credentials, `--provider`, `--local-only`, RAM ladders | `references/setup-providers.md` |
| `media-use generate`, HeyGen transcription, prices | `references/heygen.md` |
| Fades, gain, ducking, automation, effects, buses on placed tracks | `references/mixing.md` |
| Local commands: grade, cutout, loudness, beats | `references/local.md` |
| Remembered preferences | `references/memory.md` |

# SKILL ORIGINAL: captions-overlay

# Captions Overlay Doctrine

> **Overlay doctrine — supplements the upstream `/hyperframes` skill's `references/captions.md`. Applies ON TOP of it; do not expect it folded into the upstream skill.**

Two ideas combine here. First, the **caption model** — every spoken phrase is `drop`,
`rail`, or `embed`, and embed is the scarce earned peak, not the default. Second, the
**overlay law** — a caption line is composited ON TOP of the film as an overlay; it is
NOT a reserved zone, so you never shift content up or leave a dead band to "make room"
for it. The two reinforce each other: because captions ride as an overlay (the verbatim
rail in front, the occasional embed behind the subject), the composition keeps its full
frame and centers on the true vertical center.

## The caption model — drop / rail / embed

Every spoken phrase is one of three things:

|           | What                                             | How it's shown                                                                                                                                                    |
| --------- | ------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **drop**  | filler — um/uh, stutters, self-corrections       | not shown                                                                                                                                                         |
| **rail**  | the default — ordinary spoken content (verbatim) | clean lower-third subtitle, **in front**, readable. A punch word can get an inline `emphasis` highlight (accent colour / active-word pop) — it stays on the rail. |
| **embed** | a promoted peak — the headline beat              | one big word composited **behind the subject** (matte occlusion), designed entrance + exit                                                                        |

**The rail carries most of the text; embed is the scarce, earned peak** — ≤1 per beat,
never two adjacent/co-visible, spaced ≥ a beat apart. A short clip → usually one embed;
a long explainer → ~one per section. Embedding every word is the common mistake.

This is the default shape (rail = the verbatim lower-third; embed = the climax
composited behind the subject). A pure-cinematic treatment drops the rail and makes
everything embed-style — use it only for pure-cinematic asks, never for explainer /
voiceover where the words must read.

### Rail-first, embed-scarce (the load-bearing rules)

- **Rail-first for talking-head / explainer.** Don't embed the whole transcript — most
  text is the rail; embed only peaks. Embedding everything is the default mistake.
- **Embed is scarce + spaced.** ≤1 embed per sentence/beat, never two adjacent or
  co-visible, ≥ a beat apart, at most one `apex`. climax = per-beat peak, **not** "the
  single payoff of the entire clip."

## The overlay law — captions are NOT a reserved band

In a composed video with captions enabled, the caption line is a **small, minimal
word-by-word line** composited as an overlay layer ON TOP of the whole film (a single
text line, bottom-centered, roughly the bottom ~5-8% of canvas height). It is an
overlay, not a reserved zone:

- **Center the composition on the TRUE vertical center — y = H / 2** (landscape 540,
  portrait 960). Do not shift content up to "make room" for captions; a composition
  centered at 0.42 × H with a dead lower band is the bug, not the fix.
- Content may extend to the canvas bottom. Full-bleed subjects, rails, and backgrounds
  all welcome.
- **One soft courtesy rule:** avoid parking _critical small readable text_ (a URL line,
  a legal line, a sub-caption) exactly in the bottom ~80px center span where the caption
  line sits — the overlay would fight it. Large imagery / cards / ambient content under
  the captions is fine; the caption skin is designed to read over content.
- There is no machine keep-out gate (the old `captions.mjs keepout` check is retired).
  Snapshot review judges caption-over-content legibility visually.

**When captions are disabled:** identical positioning freedom — the overlay simply
doesn't exist.

## Why these two rules are one doctrine

The model says the rail rides **in front** and an embed is a rare word composited
**behind the subject** — both are layers added to footage that ships untouched. The
overlay law says the caption line is a layer composited **on top** of the whole film,
not a band carved out of the layout. So whether captions go over footage or over a
composed film, they are an overlay you add, not a zone you reserve:

- Keep the full frame; center on true center; let content run to the edges.
- Make the rail (or the small overlay caption line) carry the verbatim words.
- Promote a word to an embed only at a genuine peak — scarce, spaced, never two at once.
- Reserve nothing; judge legibility of captions-over-content visually, not by a keep-out gate.

# SKILL ORIGINAL: oversized-cursor

# Oversized Cursor — the eye-carrier

A deliberately oversized macOS-style pointer that travels the frame as a _visible
protagonist_: it enters from off-screen, walks the viewer's eye to the next point of
interest, clicks to cause the next thing that happens, and leaves. Production-proven
across multiple launch films.

**Why it exists.** Big cursor movement is one of the cheapest high-yield motion sources
in a launch video: one element, transform-only tweens, and it (1) brings the eye across
the screen on scenes that would otherwise read as dead, (2) gives causal ignition to
morphs/transitions ("the click did that"), and (3) segments the eye out of a stale
state when kicking off a new scene or a complex animation sequence. Bigger is better —
an actual-size cursor disappears at video scale.

## Size & look (house convention)

- **Full-frame scenes: `7cqw`** (≈134px at 1920). In-mock / small-frame variants:
  `4.6–5.5cqw`. Never smaller.
- One SVG arrow geometry everywhere. Two proven fills — white body + black stroke, or
  black body (`#1c1c1c`) + white stroke (1.4px). Pick per scene contrast, keep it
  constant per film.
- **Brand-motif cursors (the power play).** The macOS arrow is the DEFAULT, not a
  mandate. When the subject brand has a recognizable cursor identity — a collaborative
  design tool's colored multiplayer arrow with a name tag (Figma-style), a creative
  suite's precision crosshair, a distinctive product pointer — use THAT cursor instead:
  instantly legible brand language for anyone who knows the product. Same laws apply
  unchanged (oversized scale, physical entry/exit, tip-targeting, click-ignition), and
  a name-tag variant travels as one rigid unit (tag trailing the arrow). Reach for it
  only when the motif is genuinely referenceable; a cursor nobody recognizes is just a
  weird arrow — default back to macOS.
- `filter: drop-shadow(0 4px 6px rgba(0,0,0,.3))`, `pointer-events: none`,
  `z-index` above all scene content, `will-change: transform`.

```css
#root .cursor {
  position: absolute;
  left: 48%;
  top: 115%; /* off-screen below — the resting pose IS off-screen */
  width: 7cqw;
  height: 7cqw;
  z-index: 20;
  filter: drop-shadow(0 4px 6px rgba(0, 0, 0, 0.3));
  pointer-events: none;
  will-change: transform;
}
```

## Entry law — physical, never revealed

The cursor **always enters from off-screen** (canonical: from below, `top:115–120%`)
and travels to its first target in one decelerating glide. It must _feel like it
entered the room_. Never opacity-fade it in at a resting position, never mask-reveal
it — that reads as a glitch (a real, repeatedly observed failure mode).

- Default path: **straight up the y-axis** to the target — no fragmented diagonals.
  A diagonal is fine when it IS the story (entering toward an off-axis target), but it
  is one continuous vector either way.
- `duration: 0.4–0.92s`, `ease: power3.out`, `immediateRender: false` on the fromTo.

```js
tl.fromTo(
  cursor,
  { left: "48.6%", top: "115%" },
  { left: "48.6%", top: "55%", duration: 0.85, ease: "power3.out", immediateRender: false },
  0.25,
);
```

## Tip-targeting & the click tap

The hot-spot is the arrow TIP, not the box center. Land the **tip** on the target's
center, and pivot all press scaling on the tip: `transformOrigin: '21% 14%'` (for the
house arrow path in a 24-unit viewBox).

Click = asymmetric compress/expand (1:2 ratio reads as a real tap):

```js
tl.to(cursor, { scale: 0.84, duration: 0.1, ease: "power2.in", transformOrigin: "21% 14%" }, t);
tl.to(
  cursor,
  { scale: 1, duration: 0.22, ease: "power2.out", transformOrigin: "21% 14%" },
  t + 0.1,
);
```

**The target's reaction is a separate, parallel tween** (button: `scale: 0.94` + press
color/shadow, starting at the same `t`). Cursor-only taps (e.g. focusing a text input)
get NO target reaction. Pair with `cursor-click-ripple` / `press-release-spring` for
the target side.

## The click IGNITES the next beat

Never let a morph, typing run, window transform, or scene-defining animation simply
_start_. Park the cursor on the trigger and let the click cause it, same-frame:

- click ▸ menu/submenu cascade, toggle flip
- click ▸ typing kickoff into an input
- click ▸ composer morph-down / window shrink
- click ▸ logo ignition / flight launch
- click ▸ play-state flip + UI-life wake in a product mock

During long beats it doesn't own (typing, narration), the cursor **drifts aside**
(0.5–0.9s, `power2.out`) — never sits frozen on top of the action, never wobbles idly.

## Exit law & cross-scene handoff

Two sanctioned exits — both physical, **never an opacity fade in place**:

1. **Leave the frame**: accelerate off the nearest edge with `power2.in`
   (`left:'118%'`, `left:'-12%'`, or `top:'116%'`), 0.5–0.7s.
2. **Cut-the-curve handoff**: in the final ~0.3s before a hard cut, the cursor starts
   accelerating (`power2.in`) toward the NEXT scene's first click point, covering the
   first ~1/3 of that path; the next composition `gsap.set`s the cursor at the
   handoff pose and continues with `power2.out` at matched velocity. The cursor itself
   becomes the carrier element that stitches the seam:

```js
// scene A, last 0.3s — start the journey:
tl.to(cursor, { left: "40.7%", top: "63.7%", duration: 0.3, ease: "power2.in" }, CUT - 0.3);
// scene B, t=0 — finish it at matched velocity:
gsap.set(cursorB, { left: "40.7%", top: "63.7%" });
tl.to(cursorB, { left: "22%", top: "45%", duration: 0.6, ease: "power2.out" }, 0);
```

## Checklist

- [ ] ≥ 7cqw full-frame (4.6–5.5cqw inside a mock) — when unsure, bigger
- [ ] enters from off-screen on one continuous vector (no fade/mask reveal)
- [ ] tip lands on the target center; press pivots on `transformOrigin: '21% 14%'`
- [ ] every click causes something, same-frame
- [ ] drifts aside during beats it doesn't own; zero idle wobble
- [ ] exits physically (off-frame or cut-the-curve handoff) — no fade-in-place

# SKILL ORIGINAL: seam-craft

# Seam Craft — render prerequisites for scene-to-scene transitions

This is the **render-correctness doctrine** for PLV scene-to-scene seams: the
prerequisites and master-timeline mechanics that make any transition composite
correctly, independent of which specific transition is chosen. The per-transition
catalog (crossfade, push-slide, zoom-through, cut-the-curve, …) lives in the
transition registry — this page is the doctrine that sits underneath all of them.

The transitions this doctrine governs are **Tier-B-ready**: pure transform / opacity /
filter on the two scene **clip wrappers** (`#el-<sid>`), no injected overlay DOM, no
per-scene cooperation. Overlay families (staggered blocks, blinds, light leak, grid
dissolve, page burn) and shader transitions are deferred to later phases.

## Stage ground prerequisite (white-flash guard)

Several templates open a window where the two wrappers' summed opacity < 1 (the
cut-the-curve mid-window cut, zoom-through's 0.15 floor, plain crossfade's
power-curve dip). Whatever is BEHIND the wrappers shows through during that
window. If the assembled `index.html` `#root` has no opaque background, the
renderer composites the dip over its default **white** page → a white flash at
every seam, glaring on dark films (observed on two Spotify runs before the fix).
**The assembler must paint the stage:** `#root { background:
var(--canvas-deep, var(--canvas, #000)) }` — `assemble-index.mjs` now emits this;
any other consumer of these templates owns the same guarantee.

## How the injector applies a transition

At a `break` boundary between scene _i_ (`from`) and scene _i+1_ (`to`), the
injector:

1. Extends `#el-<from>` wrapper `data-duration` by `duration_s` (holds its final
   frame — verified: `core/src/runtime/init.ts:1393-1410` external-slot branch).
2. Pulls `#el-<to>` wrapper `data-start` earlier by `duration_s` (creates the
   overlap window).
3. Reassigns **all** clip `data-track-index` as a 0/1 ping-pong so the two
   overlapping wrappers never share a track (same-track overlap is illegal —
   `core/src/lint/rules/composition.ts`). Higher track composites on top.
4. Stamps the `gsap_template` into `window.__timelines["main"]` at `T = overlap-start`.

Verified by prototype render (2026-05-31): the master-timeline wrapper tween is
seeked and rendered (no double-seek with the sub-comp's own paused timeline —
the runtime drives them independently), the extended wrapper holds scene _i_'s
final frame, and the higher-track incoming wrapper composites over + blends with
the outgoing one.

## Template placeholders

The injector substitutes these tokens in each `gsap_template` line:

| Token                              | Meaning                                                                  |
| ---------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| `__OLD__`                          | `"#el-<from>"` — outgoing clip wrapper selector (quoted)                 |
| `__NEW__`                          | `"#el-<to>"` — incoming clip wrapper selector (quoted)                   |
| `__T__`                            | overlap-start time in seconds (master clock)                             |
| `__DUR__`                          | `duration_s` for this boundary                                           |
| `__DX__`                           | horizontal travel for directional types: `-1920` (LEFT) / `1920` (RIGHT) |
| `__DY__`                           | vertical travel: `-1080` (UP) / `1080` (DOWN)                            |
| `__ORIGIN_OUT__` / `__ORIGIN_IN__` | transformOrigin pair for `squeeze`                                       |

`filter` / `scaleX` / `transformOrigin` are lint-clean on the master timeline
(verified: `core/src/lint/rules/gsap.ts` has no per-property whitelist and scopes
its checks to `data-composition-id` ranges; the x/y/scale/rotation/opacity
whitelist is a _scene-worker_ prompt rule only — it does not bind index.html).

# SKILL ORIGINAL: remotion-motion-graphics

# Remotion Motion Graphics

Remotion renders React components frame-by-frame into video. Code quality is not the
bottleneck — **motion design craft is**. Untrained generation produces linear easing,
opacity-only fades, simultaneous entrances, flat colors, and no texture. That is the
"generic AI video" look. This skill exists to prevent it.

## Non-negotiable rules (apply to EVERY composition)

1. **NEVER use linear interpolation.** Every `interpolate()` gets an easing curve;
   every entrance prefers `spring()`. Always `extrapolateLeft/Right: "clamp"`.
2. **Entrances animate 2–3 properties together** (opacity + translateY + scale).
   A lone fade is forbidden.
3. **Stagger everything.** Lists, words, rays, cards: 3–6 frame offsets. Nothing
   enters simultaneously.
4. **Exits exist and are faster than entrances** (~10 frames vs ~20).
5. **Five-layer stack in every scene**, bottom to top: background mesh → assets →
   graphics/type → color grade → grain + vignette. Never a flat solid background.
6. **Every still image gets Ken Burns** (slow scale 1→1.08 + pan). Every video asset
   uses `<OffthreadVideo>`, never `<Video>`.
7. **Idle elements breathe**: anything on screen >2s gets sin-wave micro-motion.
8. **All timing derives from `fps`** via `useVideoConfig()`. No magic frame numbers.
9. **One theme object** at the top of the project (colors, easings, spring presets,
   fonts). Never inline a hex color or easing in a component.
10. **Render, extract frames, LOOK at them, fix, re-render.** Never deliver an
    unverified render. This loop is mandatory — see Verification below.

## Workflow

### Step 1 — Scope
Determine: duration, fps (30 default; 60 only for heavy fast motion), dimensions
(1080×1920 Reels/Shorts, 1920×1080 landscape), what assets exist (images, footage,
audio, logos), and whether this is a new composition or an edit to an existing project.
If editing an existing project: read `src/` fully first, find the theme (or create one),
and refactor violations of the rules above before adding features.

### Step 2 — Setup
New project:
```bash
npm install remotion @remotion/cli react react-dom
# optional: @remotion/transitions @remotion/motion-blur @remotion/google-fonts
```
Copy `assets/theme.ts` from this skill into `src/theme.ts` and adjust the palette to
the user's brand. Structure: `src/index.ts` (registerRoot) → `src/Root.tsx`
(Composition, duration/fps/size) → `src/scenes/*.tsx` → `src/components/*.tsx`.
User assets go in `public/`, loaded via `staticFile()`.

### Step 3 — Build
Read `references/motion-patterns.md` for the reusable component implementations
(BgMesh, Grade, Grain, Vignette, KenBurns, WordReveal, Stagger, Counter, Spark,
transitions, parallax). Compose scenes from those patterns. For pacing, rhythm,
typography, color palettes, and sound design rules, read
`references/design-rules.md`.

Scene rhythm: HIT → hold (15–20 still frames) → build → HIT. Something must move
in the first 15 frames. Never >90 frames without a new visual element.

### Step 4 — Render
```bash
npx remotion render src/index.ts <CompId> out/video.mp4 --codec h264 --crf 17
```
Remotion needs a Chromium binary. If its auto-download fails (sandboxes, offline CI),
find one and pass it explicitly:
```bash
which chromium chromium-browser google-chrome 2>/dev/null
ls /opt/pw-browsers 2>/dev/null   # Playwright installs live here
npx remotion render ... --browser-executable=<path>
```
If full Chrome errors with "Old Headless mode has been removed", use a
`headless_shell` binary instead (Playwright ships one as
`chromium_headless_shell-*/chrome-linux/headless_shell`).

### Step 5 — VERIFY (mandatory, never skip)
Extract frames at key moments and visually inspect each one. The portable
method — works on machines with no system ffmpeg, renders the exact frame:
```bash
for f in 15 45 90 150; do
  npx remotion still src/index.ts <CompId> out/check_$f.png --frame $f --overwrite
done
```
If system ffmpeg IS available, extracting from the finished mp4 also verifies
the encode itself (`-ss` seeking is more portable than `select=` filters, whose
quoting breaks in some shells and in Remotion's bundled ffmpeg):
```bash
ffmpeg -v error -ss 1.5 -i out/video.mp4 -frames:v 1 check_1.png
```
Look for, and fix, in order of frequency:
- **Spacing bugs**: `gap`/`margin` in `em` resolves against the PARENT font-size
  (often 16px), not the text size — use px values in flex containers around big type.
- Text overflowing or touching frame edges (keep critical content in middle 75%
  vertically for 9:16 — platform UI covers top/bottom).
- Elements visible before their entrance or after their exit (missing clamp).
- Color/contrast failures: hero color on >1 element per frame, dim text unreadable
  over the grade.
- Layer order mistakes (grain/vignette must be on top, grade above content).
Fix → re-render → re-extract → re-inspect. Only deliver after a clean pass.
Then run the final checklist at the bottom of `references/design-rules.md`.

### Editing the user's existing footage
To enhance an existing mp4 (captions, grade, intro/outro) rather than build from
scratch: put the file in `public/`, render it as the asset layer with
`<OffthreadVideo src={staticFile("clip.mp4")} />`, set composition duration from the
clip length, and stack graphics/grade/grain above it. Get the clip's duration and fps
with `ffprobe` before setting up the composition. For word-synced captions over
speech, see the captions section of `references/motion-patterns.md`.

## Reference files
- `references/motion-patterns.md` — copy-paste component library: backgrounds,
  grade/grain/vignette, Ken Burns, text reveals, counters, transitions, parallax,
  audio sync, captions. Read before writing components.
- `references/design-rules.md` — palettes, typography rules, scene architecture,
  sound design, pre-delivery checklist. Read before designing scenes and before
  final delivery.
- `assets/theme.ts` — the theme template to copy into every project.

## Common failure modes to actively avoid
- **Emoji as icons.** Emoji render as full-color platform glyphs (green ✳️,
  blue 🌐) that ignore your palette and silently break the one-hero-color rule,
  and they sit on whatever background you gave them (orange mascot on orange
  tile = invisible). Draw glyphs with CSS/SVG in theme colors, or verify every
  emoji against the extracted frames.
- **No SFX assets is not a reason to ship silent.** Synthesize a minimal kit as
  16-bit WAVs from a Node script (noise-burst whoosh, pitch-drop pop, sine-thump
  kick/bass, detuned-sine pad) into `public/sfx/` — see `examples/scripts/` in
  the repo. Zero downloads, fully deterministic.
- Generating one giant component instead of themed, reusable pieces.
- `durationInFrames` mismatch between Composition and scene content (dead air).
- Forgetting `--overwrite` on re-renders, then inspecting the stale file.
- Fonts: never rely on system defaults for hero text; load a display font via
  `@remotion/google-fonts` or `@font-face` + `staticFile`.
- Trying to "describe" the result to the user instead of rendering and verifying it.

# SKILL ORIGINAL: remotion-3d-ticker

# Remotion 3D Vertical Ticker

This skill provides a reusable architectural pattern and component for creating infinite, multi-column 3D scrolling animations (often used for photo galleries, 3D照片滚动墙, tech-stack showcases, or credit rolls) in Remotion.

## How it works

The infinite scrolling illusion is achieved using **domain duplication and modular arithmetic**, combined with CSS 3D transforms:

1. **The Math of Infinity:** We duplicate the content list (`[...items, ...items]`). We calculate a \`progress\` from 0 to 1 based on the frame rate and desired loop duration. We translate the column from \`0%\` to \`-50%\` (or \`-50%\` to \`0%\` for reverse). Because the content is duplicated, the frame at \`-50%\` looks identical to the frame at \`0%\`, creating a seamless loop when it resets.
2. **3D Perspective:** A parent wrapper applies \`perspective: 1000px\`, \`rotateX(20deg)\`, and \`scale(1.2)\`. The scale is crucial to ensure the tilted top and bottom edges stretch beyond the 2D screen bounds, preventing empty background bleeding.
3. **Masking:** CSS \`linear-gradient\` overlays are placed absolutely at the top and bottom with a high \`zIndex\` to smoothly fade the content into the background color.

## How to use this skill

When a user requests a vertical scrolling gallery or infinite ticker:

1. **Copy the asset:** Provide or copy the generic component located at `assets/VerticalTicker.tsx` into their project's components/animations folder.
2. **Configure:** Guide the user to import and render the `<VerticalTicker />` component within their Remotion `<Composition />`.
3. **Adapt Data:** The component is data-agnostic. It accepts any array of image URLs or React nodes as columns. You can specify different \`durationInSeconds\` and \`direction\` (1 or -1) per column to achieve parallax.

## Provided Assets

- `assets/VerticalTicker.tsx`: The highly reusable, strongly-typed Remotion component implementing the 3D infinite scroll pattern.

---

# SKILL ORIGINAL: remotion-to-hyperframes

**Plugin installs:** Inside a HyperFrames plugin, follow [plugin execution rules](../hyperframes/references/plugin-installation.md).

**Usage beacon:** run `npx hyperframes events --skill=remotion-to-hyperframes` once as this skill starts. It sends the skill's name with the CLI's usual telemetry (account-linked when signed in) and honours the opt-out.

# Import into HyperFrames

HyperFrames is the container: its tracks, sub-compositions and variables are editable in Studio whatever runs inside a clip. Bring each part in at the highest level it supports and record which level you used.

| Level | For | How | Editable in Studio afterwards |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Native | code that can become HTML + GSAP: most Remotion, HTML/CSS/GSAP animation, simple Motion Canvas | translate it, then verify frame by frame | every element; variables, catalog, presets apply |
| Hosted | code that can draw any requested frame in a browser: canvas, p5.js, Three.js, Lottie, WebGL, other JS animation | keep the code and drive it from HyperFrames time | timing, position, exposed parameters |
| Media | anything that outputs video or frames: Manim, After Effects, Blender, PIL, moviepy | render to a file and use it as a clip | cut, place, layer; not the pixels inside |

Code that depends on wall-clock time, live physics or the network can only come in as media.

## Native: translate, then verify

1. For Remotion, run `python3 <SKILL_DIR>/scripts/lint_source.py <source dir>` first. Blockers (`useState`, `useReducer`, `useEffect` with dependencies, async `calculateMetadata`, React UI kits such as MUI, Chakra, Mantine, antd, shadcn, Radix, NextUI) don't translate: host them or refactor them out, never approximate (`references/escape-hatch.md`). Warnings (`@remotion/lambda` config, `delayRender`, `useMemo`, custom hooks) are dropped and noted.
2. Translate to the `/hyperframes` contract: one sub-composition per scene, times in seconds (`frame / fps`), asset paths root-relative. Each Remotion API's equivalent is in `references/api-map.md`. Put Remotion's render-page rule `* { box-sizing: border-box; }` once in the root `index.html` `<style>`: without it every bordered or padded element grows by its border and padding.
3. Keep it editable: the source's props become `data-composition-variables` bound with `data-var-text`, `data-var-src` or `var(--id)`; each `Sequence` becomes a timed clip or a sub-composition; a reused component becomes one sub-composition mounted several times with `data-variable-values`.
4. Compare frame by frame: `npx hyperframes diff <original.mp4> . --at <t,…>` scores your project against the original at those times without rendering; sample every scene and both sides of every cut. Then render and `npx hyperframes diff <original.mp4> <hf.mp4>` scores every frame: SSIM, the worst cell of each frame (one late or recoloured element), cuts, and a sheet of the worst moments (thresholds per complexity tier in `references/eval.md`). Render the Remotion side with `Config.setVideoImageFormat("png")` and `Config.setColorSpace("bt709")`, or the diff measures the encoders (about 0.05 SSIM).
5. Write what didn't translate cleanly (dropped volume ramps, approximated presentations, substituted fonts) to `TRANSLATION_NOTES.md`.

| Remotion | Read |
| --- | --- |
| `Composition`, `defaultProps`, `schema`, `calculateMetadata` | `references/parameters.md` |
| `Sequence`, `Series`, `Loop`, `AbsoluteFill`, `Freeze` | `references/sequencing.md` |
| `useCurrentFrame`, `interpolate`, `spring`, `Easing`, `interpolateColors` | `references/timing.md` |
| `Audio`, `Video`, `Img`, `IFrame`, `staticFile`, `delayRender` | `references/media.md` |
| `TransitionSeries`, `@remotion/transitions` | `references/transitions.md` |
| `@remotion/lottie` | `references/lottie.md` |
| `@remotion/google-fonts`, `Font.loadFont`, `@font-face` | `references/fonts.md` |
| What can't be ported | `references/limitations.md` |

Before hand-building a named visual the source used, search `npx hyperframes catalog --query "<the visual, in English>" --json`: it ranks the whole registry with nothing installed.

## Hosted

Keep the code and let HyperFrames own the clock: it draws the frame for the time it is given. The seek protocol and the built-in adapters for Lottie, Three.js, anime.js, WAAPI and CSS animations are in `../hyperframes/references/runtimes.md`. No `requestAnimationFrame` loop or timer as the time source, seeded randomness only, and `data-duration` on the root when nothing else defines the length.

## Media

`<video class="clip" data-has-audio="true" src="assets/x.mp4" ...>`, or `muted` when it is silent. Nothing else is needed.

In the final message, list each imported part with its level, what a person can change in Studio, and how the frame-by-frame comparison came out.
